#2746. Zeek Biggers suffers season-ending injury; Could Christian Wilkins be a consideration?

The Miami Dolphins defensive line suffered a major loss last week when Zach Sieler was placed on injured reserve following a biceps rupture sustained in the team's game against the Detroit Lions. A week later, they lost another key defensive tackle with Zeek Biggers sustaining a knee injury that will end his season. NFL Network's Ian Rapoport was the first to report the update.

Biggers will undergo surgery to repair the lateral meniscus in his right knee. He is expected to fully recover in time to start the 2027 season.

The Dolphins selected Biggers in the seventh round of the 2025 NFL Draft. He has appeared in 18 games, including all nine for Miami this season, with two starts and has recorded 38 tackles, 2.5 sacks, and a pass defensed in his career.

With Sieler on injured reserve and Biggers headed there, Miami is left with defensive tackles Jordan Phillips, Kenneth Grant, and Keith Cooper, Jr., on the 53-man roster. They also have Jordan Jefferson on the practice squad.

Phillips also left last Sunday's game against the Indianapolis Colts and had his arm in a sling after the game. He was expected to get an MRI on Monday to determine the severity of his injury.

The Dolphins could be looking to bring in a veteran defensive tackle to provide depth with he loss of Biggers, and could be desperate for additional linemen if Phillips is also unavailable for an extended period of time.

Miami could consider former Dolphins defensive tackle Christian Wilkins, if the veteran is healthy after a foot injury cost him most of 2024 and all of 2025. The former 13th overall pick spent five years in Miami before joining the Las Vegas Raiders in 2024. There was drama between Wilkins and the Raiders following his injury, with reported disagreements about his recovery plan as well as allegation that he tried to kiss a teammate on the head. Wilkins has not played since being released.

#2745. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs Detroit Pistons

I know that (as my colleague Charlie Thaddeus pointed out) there's a tendency to embellish in the basketball blogosphere and on social media, but I feel like the significance of this win streak has really snuck up on us all.

And I get it. It's a weak portion of the schedule, no doubt about it. The Spurs have only played four teams currently at .500 or better during this run, with the benefit of some injury absences.

And yes, it's very early in the season, as opposed to when everyone else in rhythm and fighting for seeding and playoff/play-in berths. The competitive desperation just isn't on the same level.

Still, that's five out of twelve games, and you can really only play the teams in front of you. That's just how it is.

Consider for a moment that with a win against the Pistons, this win streak would tie for 4th longest in franchise history.

Do you know how many times the Spurs won 13+ games in the Duncan era? Just five times.

How about the David Robinson era? Just four.

[Side note: How crazy is it that the combination of Duncan and Robinson only resulted in one 13+ game win streak?!]

Consider that if the Spurs win this game, their next three games will be against three of the bottom four teams in the Western Conference (The Clippers, The Pelicans, and The Kings).

They will have a very good shot at extending this streak to 16 games, which would be the 3rd longest streak in Spurs team history.

Tim and David only managed to win 16+ consecutive games once each.*

(*In the regular season. Duncan did it twice if you count the combined 20-game win streak in 2012 from April 12th to the end of Game 2 of the Western Conference Finals. Personally, I try to forget that one.)

Consider that the Spurs had two(!) separate 11-game win streaks last season, and went undefeated over the month of February, and still couldn't get to 12, much less 13.

Consider that they've done this without the debut of an important off-season addition in Tobias Harris, and with short absences from both De'Aaron Fox and Devin Vassell.

It might not feel like it, but all things considered, this streak is a pretty big deal.

The Pistons could give them real trouble, though.

While they don't have as explosive an offense as the Spurs (who are 4th in points, 3rd in Offensive Rating vs. 12th and 13th), the Pistons rebound well enough (13th), pass well enough (10th in assists, 9th in assist percentage), and both avoid turnovers (7th in turnover percentage and assist-to-turnover ration) and force them (5th in steals, 2nd in blocks) to such an extent that they really have no glaring weaknesses to exploit.

The Spurs will have to play patient, tough, mistake free basketball against them more than most.

Even the Pistons' three-point shooting can take advantage of San Antonio sloppiness, as they sit in the top 10 in three-point percentage (9th), and their Defensive Rating is only a few ticks behind San Antonio's (6th vs. 3rd).

Yes, the Spurs will have to play exceptional ball to put Detroit away. The good news is that they have been.

Over the last 12 games the Spurs are 2nd in points scored, 1st in +/-, 1st in limiting fouls, 1st in limiting turnovers (shocking, I know), 1st in rebounds, 1st in blocks, 1st in offensive rating, 1st in net rating, 1st in player impact estimate, 2nd in 2nd chance points, 2nd in fast-break points, 1st in points in the paint, 1st in limiting points off of turnovers, and 5th in field-goal percentage. *whew*

And all of that in spite of being 23rd in three-pointers made, 15th in three-point percentage, 13th in effective field goal percentage, and 13th in free-throw attempt rate.

Basically, you have to pray that this team has a cold shooting night.

And (as the Mavericks recently learned) that still might not be enough. God forbid they starting hitting their open looks (they're near the bottom of the league in actual shooting vs quality of looks).

I know they made the Finals last season, but I just cannot emphasize how special this Spurs team really is.

If they get even a little hot from outside, my money's on them.

But my money would be on them anyway. They're just that good.

Spurs Injuries: Tobias Harris: Out (Calf), Jayden Quaintance: Out (Knee)

Pistons Injuries: Jalen Duren: Out (Hamstring), Duncan Robinson: Out (Quad), Isaiah Joe: Doubtful (Hip)

#2744. Dan Campbell: I'm not telling players to stop celebrating because of Aidan Hutchinson's injury

Lions head coach Dan Campbell isn’t going to tell his players to stop celebrating in the wake of defensive end Aidan Hutchinson’s torn ACL.

Hutchinson will miss the rest of the season after injuring his right knee while celebrating a sack of Drake Maye in Sunday’s game. Hutchinson jumped in the air and immediately grabbed his right knee after suffering the injury when he landed back on the turf in Munich.

During an appearance on 97.1 The Ticket on Tuesday, Campbell called the injury “one of those things that happened” and that “this game is built off of emotion” that he doesn’t want to take away from his players.

“I hate it, I know he hates it. But I mean, the number of moves that I’ve seen Hutch make and the number of plyometrics, explosive movements, the spins, the one leg lateral movements, lateral balances, I’m just like, it was one of those things, man,” Campbell said. “I hate it. I think we all understand what it is, and let’s be smart, but no, I’m not gonna say, ‘Nobody celebrates anymore.’”

Campbell recounted a story from his own playing days when he tore his biceps while celebrating a play and that didn’t lead him to make celebration avoidance a rule for the Lions when he got the job. That doesn’t make Hutchinson’s injury any easier for the Lions to deal with, but it makes his response to questions about the advice he’ll give in the future easier to understand.

#2740. What Jack Nicklaus used to do before even hitting a shot which amateurs should copy

Legendary caddie Billy Foster has revealed the tip most amateur golfers should use to line themselves up correctly, as he detailed the mistake he sees so many players make, particularly on the tee.

Correct alignment should arguably be one of the simplest tasks for every player, whether you are a scratch golfer or someone with a high handicap. However, most players know the feeling of being left baffled by the line your ball is travelling on despite hitting exactly the shot you wanted.

Of course, there can be a tendency to overcompensate with the stance for those players who can hit a wild hook or slice. But what does not help, regardless of what shot you expect to hit, is having little clue about what target line you are setting up to.

Speaking in a video for Your Golf NCG, Foster explained the error he sees so many amateur golfers make, and shared the simple advice that can help them out enormously.

"You'd always see the likes of Justin Rose or Jack Nicklaus, even, they'd always stand behind it and you'll see them doing that [raising the club in the air to line up the ball and the target line]. They're choosing something, it might be four, five, six yards in front of themselves. It might be a divot, it might be an old tee peg, it might be a leaf or something, but they'll pick something on the line where they want to hit it. So when they're stepping into the shot [they're lined up correctly]. The biggest mistake amateurs make for alignment is shocking. I've caddied in probably 2000 pro-ams and they'll get on here and they'll go, 'alright, I'm going down there', and you're looking behind and they're aiming into the trees 30 yards right. So it always helps to choose something on the line a few yards in front of you where you want to hit it," he said.

"I'll be looking, let's say that left-hand divot. I'd tee it up so that left-hand divot is right on my line, so then when I step into it, I'll point the club face at the divot and then stand square to the divot. Then I know I'm aiming correctly.

"I'm not saying your great golfers [do that], but that's where most amateurs go wrong. They don't have attention to detail of their aiming point."

Good course management certainly appears to be low-hanging fruit for a lot of amateur golfers looking to lower their scores. Understanding your game and making the most of the skills you do have is clearly going to have a positive impact on the scorecard.

Obviously, the worry for some players is that they are putting a limit on how good they can become if they never take on the hero shot. But there are times when Foster believes that most would benefit from the lower-risk approach, such as when faced with a 50-yard pitch shot.

It can be tempting to use plenty of loft to try and carry the ball most of the way. But Foster suggested that many would be better off trying to treat the shot much more like a putt.

"A lot of these lads, they just get the lob wedge out every time. And you're relying on a decent bounce, is it going to spin? Is it going to spin sidewards? Is it going to bounce too hard? I think when you control your wedges, especially with chips around the green, obviously that was miles better, the second one, because there's less loft, because you are controlling your ball flight, bringing it down, and you're going to get a more consistent skip to the hole. Sometimes you've got to see less loft and see it landing and running a bit. You've got to get the lob wedge absolutely perfect," he said.

Foster has probably seen absolutely everything in the game, having caddied for the likes of Seve Ballesteros, Lee Westwood, and Matt Fitzpatrick. He even caddied for Tiger Woods at one stage.

So while it is not always easy to make the right decision when the opportunity presents itself to open the club face and take on the high-tariff shot, it is probably worth remembering that Foster will have a better idea than most of just how likely it is that most players will not be able to pull it off.

#2739. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2738. The 49ers are proving doubters wrong about their Kaelon Black draft selection

The San Francisco 49ers made a couple of bold swings in the 2026 NFL Draft, taking wide receiver De'Zhaun Stribling and running back Kaelon Black well above consensus this offseason.

While the Stribling pick caught a lot of initial flak, the Black pick drew the ire of national media as well, as he was the third running back taken in the draft, with Kyle Shanahan coming out and even saying that the Indiana product was the No. 2 back on their board.

The 49ers have had their fair share of misses in the draft at running back under Shanahan and John Lynch. Their first pick at the position back in 2017 was Joe Williams, who evidently didn't pan out. From there, they've taken Trey Sermon, Elijah Mitchell, Ty Davis-Price, Isaac Guerendo, and Jordan James before taking Black this offseason.

Williams, Sermon, Davis-Price, and Guerendo all failed to pan out and were third or fourth-round picks, which added a stain to the regime's draft history at the position. But Black has been an immediate success, surpassing my expectations from the draft, and the 49ers have shown a clear trust in the rookie from Day 1.

Black has gone for 356 yards and two touchdowns on 79 carries, providing a good complement to Christian McCaffrey as a hard-nosed, rugged runner. His 4.5 yards per carry mark ranks 12th in the NFL, while he's averaged nearly 10 carries per game.

Black has really come along late. He went for 63 yards and a touchdown on 5.3 yards per carry against Atlanta (although he did fumble in that game). He followed that with 70 yards on 11 carries last weekend against Las Vegas. And he just had 53 yards and a touchdown on 4.8 yards per carry against Dallas this weekend.

San Francisco came into the year saying it wanted to keep Christian McCaffrey fresh throughout the season. It's followed through with that sentiment, as McCaffrey is averaging 12.8 carries per game so far. A year ago, he averaged 18.6 carries per game through nine games. And that's not including the high receiving volume he's had in both seasons. Last season, the 49ers star paced the NFL in touches by over 40. This year, he's 17th, having nearly 90 touches less than the NFL's leader Jahmyr Gibbs.

The freshness has also led to a far more efficient backfield. The 49ers are fourth in EPA per rush this season and fourth in rushing success rate. A part of that is the offensive line play and a part of that is the ability to rotate backs and have that trust throughout games.

Speaking on the backfield split, head coach Kyle Shanahan pointed out how valuable McCaffrey has been as a mentor to Black this season, which has led to a successful pairing.

"I think Christian's the perfect mentor just because even if he doesn't say anything, just how Christian works every second, Christian's the most detailed, focused, obsessive player I've ever been around," Shanahan said on Monday. "Just watch how he blocks when [WR] Deebo's [Samuel Sr.] running back and he's playing receiver and just the technique and the effort he goes through on every single play. Just the way Christian plays football is the best example or mentor to anybody who's on our team or anybody who wants to watch how a football player goes about his business.

"I also think Christian really respects Kaelon. I remember Christian telling me right when he got here how much of a pro that guy seemed like and seemed like he was made of the right stuff. It's because K.B., similar to Christian, he works at everything. He's very hard on himself. He's very tough and approaches each day to get better. As talents and as people, I think they pair together very well."

The two styles are quite different. McCaffrey is the elusive back who obviously flourishes as a receiver, while Black has not been afraid to exert his physicality, which came out several times against Dallas.

"Oh, it was awesome," Shanahan said of Black's physicality. "The one I got to see live was running over the safety, and the safety brought it too. I thought that was a good play by the safety, but K.B. brought it more. Then to finish it on the corner was such an impressive run. I didn't get to see his chips on the D-Lineman live.

"I saw Quinnen Williams go out in the game and they told me that was because K.B. hit him and I was trying to figure out how he did because it was on a bootleg. Then he did it another time on another player going through the B-gap, so just the physicality he looks for and then the way his body's wired that when you have that mentality and you're wired that way, obviously when he hits them it feels a little bit different."

The 49ers have been willing to lean on McCaffrey when they need to, giving him 15+ carries four times this season. But he hasn't exceeded the 20-carry mark, which has allowed him to maintain a force as a receiver, where he leads all running backs in receiving yards. We saw that play out on Sunday, as McCaffrey had 13 carries to Black's 11, but added four catches for 161 yards and two touchdowns in the air.

San Francisco's offense has continued to find new heights, and a big part of that has been Black's readiness and ability to complement McCaffrey in the backfield. Thus far, they've proved the doubters wrong with a bold draft pick who has made a huge impact as a rookie so far.

#2736. Jim Harbaugh on finally getting a win: "Let's do it every week"

Before the season, many expected the Chargers not only to get back to the playoffs for the third straight year but to finally make some noise.

Instead, it took them more than two months to make a peep.

But they roared to life on Monday night, with a win over the Ravens in Baltimore. The 23-20 victory instantly restored the boundless optimism of coach Jim Harbaugh.

“Thrilled, let’s do it every week,” Harbaugh told reporters after the game, via Jeff Legwold of ESPN. “Good to see the smiles back on the faces.”

Harbaugh should be smiling the most. His stubborn refusal to make major changes — starting with firing or reassigning offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel and continuing with a failure to trade players who won’t be there next year, like Khalil Mack — was putting Harbaugh in increasing danger of being fired. While the playoffs are a practical impossibility, Harbaugh’s obvious goal is survival for 2027.

That’s ultimately why there was no fire sale; moving good players would have made Harbaugh’s firing more likely. Surely, it’s one of the reasons why he resisted stockpiling future picks. If he’s not there to use them, who cares?

Could Harbaugh and the Chargers stack some wins? They’ve got the 2-8 Jets, the 5-5 Patriots (without quarterback Drake Maye), the 3-6 Buccaneers, and the 4-6 Raiders over the next four weeks.

It changes after that, with three of the last four games against the 8-1 49ers, the 7-2 Broncos, and the 7-2 Chiefs.

Will they run the table and sneak into the playoffs? Most likely not. Can they win enough to get Harbaugh a fourth season with the Chargers?

At this point, that’s the only realistic goal left.

#2735. Kyle Shanahan on 49ers’ defensive effort in Dallas: ‘The tape tells a different story’

For the 49ers' defense following San Francisco's shootout victory over the Dallas Cowboys, the mood in the locker room was anything but celebratory.

Having forced five turnovers in the Week 9 win over the Las Vegas Raiders, Week 10 saw the defense endure its most significant struggles of the season, giving up five touchdowns in San Francisco's 46-40 win.

The 49ers had issues getting home on pass rushes, recording only one sack of Dak Prescott, which was split between Mykel Williams and Dre Greenlaw, while a banged-up secondary struggled to contain wideout George Pickens.

As such, there was plenty of frustration among the 49er defenders following the display Raheem Morris' unit put on the field, which was articulated by defensive tackle DeForest Buckner.

"Not every win is going to be perfect, and we found a way to win [against Dallas]," Buckner told reporters.

"The offense, we had to lean on the offense heavily; we needed 46 to win the game, and they put up 46… We just got to execute all four quarters, though.

"We didn't do that, and we know that's why the win feels kind of like a loss to a bunch of us [and] we just got to continue to get better. It's not the lack of locking in. I feel like guys are like that. It's just executing each and every play to the best of our abilities, and we're not doing that on a consistent basis."

While that mindset is of no surprise to Kyle Shanahan, the 49ers head coach was keen to put the defense's efforts in perspective when asked about the win feeling like a loss on that side of the ball.

Shanahan told his Monday conference call:

"That's how I expect them to [approach it]. Anytime you come off a field and that's what the stats say, you're not going to feel good about yourself. I think the tape tells a little bit of a different story. I didn't think it was like that for three quarters and I think we kind of fell apart defensively in the fourth quarter, which made it very bad. That's why you've got to put it in perspective. No, it wasn't good and we can clean a lot of that stuff up and do better and we expect to. I felt pretty similar in the offensive standpoint, just the opposite way. If you look at the stats and the scoreboard and everything, it seems like the offense played one of their best games ever, but I didn't feel that way at all. I thought we had three big plays that were really good by all our guys, but I think you take those three plays away and there's a lot of stuff that we could have cleaned up and done better on offense."

He added: "It's a week-to-week league and really excited for that win. Never going to take that away from those guys. Even look at special teams. I thought special teams had a really good chance to really affect that game. They held a good returner in check. I thought we got some explosive returns, but we ruined them with some dumb penalties. Some stuff that we could overcome. I think we left about 70 yards out there taken back or given collectively on special teams with penalties.

"Really all three phases, it was kind of a crazy game where things just stat-wise they stuck out a ton. But, there's a lot to learn from that silent tape and not to get too caught up in it either way, good or bad."

Shanahan has long since preached the value of not getting too high after a win, and not getting too low following a defeat. While a game that saw the defense concede 40 may feel like a disaster for those on that side of the ball, Shanahan is right to stress that there were positives.

San Francisco forced two Dallas punts in the second quarter that allowed the 49ers to go into the half with a one-point lead, before the Niners in success in holding the Cowboys to a field goal on their first possession of the half and then forcing a turnover on their next series helped enable the offense to stretch the advantage to 19.

As Shanahan mentioned, a key problem for the 49er defense is that it appeared to completely run out of gas in the fourth quarter, when Dallas scored three touchdowns to keep the game competitive right until the end.

Avoiding that happening again will be critical for the Niners in Week 11 as they face the Minnesota Vikings at altitude in Mexico City in Week 11 in what projects as a massive game for seeding in the NFC playoffs.

But one disappointing performance doesn't change the fact the defense has made clear progress in 2026. For the 49ers to achieve their goal and end the postseason with the Lombardi in hand, they will need to get the pass rush going down the stretch and in the playoffs. The good news is the 49ers have the talent and the time still on their side to make those strides that could see the entire locker room enjoy the biggest of celebrations come February.

#2734. James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays ‘before they actually happen'

James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays 'before they actually happen' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Tyrese Maxey admitted that he wasn't entirely ready for LeBron James' first pass of the afternoon Sunday.

Maxey cut through the paint and James' bounce pass was perfect, but the Sixers' All-NBA guard missed his layup. He's not fully accustomed yet to James' precognitive gifts.

"There's still some times when you're surprised," Maxey said after the Sixers' win over the Pacers. "… I was cutting through just to cut and create space. The ball hit me in the chest and I missed the layup. I was like, 'Ah shoot, my bad.' He was like, 'Yeah, you weren't looking.'"

James has been trying to teach his teammates that he'll spot openings they may not notice.

"I just tell my guys, 'Always pay attention to me when I've got the ball,' because I definitely see plays develop before they happen," James said.

For the most part, James' teammates adhered to his request on the latest historic day in a milestone-filled career. By setting up VJ Edgecombe's layup with 1:19 left in the fourth quarter, James moved into third place on the NBA's all-time assists list, passing Jason Kidd.

The 22-time All-Star recorded 11 assists, his most so far as a Sixer, and now sits at 12,092 in his career. Chris Paul (12,552 assists) and John Stockton (15,806) are the only two players above James.

"It's obviously super humbling," James said. "It's something that I didn't foresee in my career, but I knew assists meant a lot to me. Just the movement of the ball, being able to find my teammates, putting the ball on time and on target.

"And also just being linked with some of the greats that have ever played this game and a dear friend of mine, J-Kidd. It's really special."

James is at or near the top of many all-time leaderboards. He's first in NBA history in points, games and minutes, sixth in steals and fifth in triple-doubles.

Passing has always been a core element of his game.

"First of all, he's got the vision and the awareness," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He's got the size to see. I think he's got some anticipation. Just a half-second before a play happens, he knows how the defense is moving. … And he throws crisp passes. Some of those are cooking out to the corner three guys, so it doesn't let the close-out get there nearly as quickly."

Whether it's a long-range rocket, a drive-and-kick dish or a cross-court feed from the post that suggests he might have eyes in the back of his head, James has always enjoyed finding a wide range of ways to help his teammates.

"For someone that talented, he's very unselfish," Maxey said. "He really prides himself on getting guys open."

As James recalled Sunday, he had exceptional passing tools long before he entered the NBA 24 seasons ago.

"Since I was a kid, I've had the ability to see plays before they actually happen," he said. "Seeing movement, seeing cracks in the defense before they were even open … it's something that's been with me since I was a kid.

"I just had that vision and it just stuck with me throughout my playing days."

#2733. Fantasy Football 2026: Waiver wire, Week 11 edition

Welcome to the Fantasy Football Waiver Wire for Week 11.

The waiver wire was a feeding frenzy last week. A fresh round of injuries, a few NFL trades that impacted rosters, four teams on bye, and a lot of attractive free agents on offer were chum in the water, and managers bit.

Well, buckle up, fantasy players, because Week 11 is the worst bye week of the season. It's the only one with six teams on bye, and you're going to hear terms like "byepocalypse". On top of that, there's less to choose from this week, in part because so many players got scooped up last week (examples: Emmett Johnson, Kaleb Johnson, and Tank Dell). But that doesn't mean there aren't players to add who can help you. I'll do my best to identify them here.

The Process: Each week, I try to identify the top Waiver Wire targets and evaluate the short- and long-term prospects of those players. I also do my best to reveal which players who are coming off a big week are fool's gold. Finally, I look at streamers who might help you in a pinch (this is usually more important during bye weeks).

Some players discussed won't be available in your league. I try to list players who are available in more than 50% of leagues. But what does that even mean? A 10-team league with six bench spots will have a much larger Free Agent pool than a 14-team league with eight. Most leagues fall somewhere in between. This isn't "one size fits all." By way of example, I won't list Kirk Cousins, Emmett Johnson, Kaleb Johnson, Antonio Williams, Jordyn Tyson, Tank Dell, or Ryan Flournoy, among others who probably aren't available in any of your leagues.

A slight change this week: this column is dropping AFTER the Week 11 Monday night game (LAC@BAL).

WEEK 11 BYES: Falcons, Browns, Packers, Rams, Patriots, Seahawks

Don't look now, but Cam Ward is starting to look more like a No. 1 draft pick and less like a lemon. He's been putting up fantasy points (46 of them over his last two games), and doing it without his No. 1 receiver, rookie Carnell Tate. Tennessee has scored at least 23 points in four straight games. We saw flashes last season, but Ward hasn't shown that he can be a consistent producer. His upcoming schedule is exceptional, with games against Dallas, Washington, Las Vegas, Indianapolis, and Detroit in the next month and a half. He's a nice add in 2-QB leagues if he's available, or if you just lost Drake Maye and need emergency help.

Fernando Mendoza (who I recommended last week) had his moments in his debut. Let's give him a break, since it was against Seattle, and next week he gets the equally tough Broncos (and he might be without Brock Bowers), rested off a bye. Not an easy way to start a career. But I think he can help you out if you need a second quarterback in a Superflex down the stretch. He's not afraid to run, which is a plus.

Need a Week 11 streamer? A lot of people do with the big bye week. It's not a great week for that. Ward is your best bet, against that atrocious Dallas defense that gave up 46 more points in Week 10. Jacoby Brissett at the Panthers is in play as well. He attempted 49 passes on Monday night while throwing for 340 yards. And I'll actually mention Justin Herbert, who gets the Jets at home.

Beyond those let me show you our bargain basement. Geno Smith (assuming he's able to play this week) is at the Chargers. J.J. McCarthy has a tough home matchup against the Jags. Baker Mayfield or Jalon Daniels at the Lions isn't terrible. Kyle McCord could have the gift of extended garbage time against backups at Buffalo, and especially with the Bills set to play the Chiefs on Thanksgiving night. That's a lot to talk yourself into. Speaking of which, Joe Milton wasn't good in his first start against the Giants, but he did run for 64 yards on nine carries and the Eagles could finally get some weapons back coming out of their bye. They host the Steelers, whose defense never got off the plane in Cincinnati on Sunday night. Hey, it's a brutal week and desperate times and all that…

Emari Demercado will be a hot add this week, as he has a good shot to be the lead back for the Texans on Thursday night in a plus-matchup against the Colts. Houston hasn't run the ball effectively all season, but with Woody Marks unlikely to get out of concussion protocol on a short week and David Montgomery iffy to return to action, this is a no-brainer, even if it's a short-term pickup. But don't go crazy with a FAAB bid.

Seth McGowan may have been dropped by many speculators after Jonathan Taylor didn't get traded. McGowan should be rostered. He saw a season-high 12 carries in Week 10 and scored a TD for the second straight week. He's part of the offense.

I can't imagine many people dropped Kyle Monangai, but since he never went on IR it's possible that some managers needed the roster space. He's a priority add if available, and a great streaming option this week against the Saints, who allow the most fantasy points to running backs. David Montgomery is probably still rostered in most places (people are stubborn with their fourth- or fifth-round picks, and he did score thrice in Week 1), but I'd look to add him if he isn't.

Backs who should be rostered: Kaelon Black, Kenneth Gainwell, Justice Hill, Samaje Perine, Dylan Sampson,and Israel Ibanikanda (only because the Cowboys have no depth at the position).

The Breakfast Club, a/k/a/ the "don't you forget about me" list: Jonathon Brooks, Jacory Croskey-Merritt, R.J. Harvey, and Tank Bigsby. Most of these are still eligible for an IR spot.

Xavier Hutchinson tops the list this week. The Texans have a lot of bodies at the position (and especially once Marvin Harrison, Jr. returns), but for now Hutchinson is operating as the No. 2 receiver and he has scored in three straight games. The Texans are airing it out (because, again, they can't run the ball and their vaunted defense is struggling), making Hutchinson an attractive option.

Pat Bryant may have made it through last week's waivers because Denver had a bye in Week 10. Bo Nix favors him, and it's unclear if Jaylen Waddle will be back in Week 11. Even with a healthy Waddle, Bryant has value. He's right there with Hutchinson for me.

Caleb Douglas made the list last week, and I'll add feature him again, even though Kyle McCord doesn't inspire a ton of confidence. Douglas is the receiver to roster from Miami. He's getting good target volume and topped 70 yards in each of the last two games. This week's game at Buffalo should have extended garbage time, as discussed above.

Need depth? I'll repeat a bunch of names I've listed in recent weeks, together with some new ones: Keenan Allen, Konata Mumpfield, Malachi Fields, Tre' Harris, Laquon Treadwell, Jordan Watkins, and Jakobi Meyers.

Is Brissett going to turn Mark Andrews into Trey McBride? No, of course not. But Andrews got onto the fantasy radar on Monday night with significant volume and production (8-82-0 and he caught all of his targets). Given the lack of quality at the position, he's worth a pickup as the Ravens don't have any reliable receivers after Zay Flowers, and you know Brissett likes to get ball to his tight end.

I'll just keep running back the same names. Mike Gesicki, Darren Waller, Tyler Higbee, and Pat Freiermuth are all boom-bust options who can get you a TD (and maybe a little more) if you hit it right. Chig Okonkwo offers more consistency. Brevyn Spann-Ford is interesting, given how much Dallas throws. Terrance Ferguson is a stash if you've got the space. Michael Mayer should see a bump in volume if Bowers misses time.

#2731. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2729. Mike Vrabel disagrees with Milton Williams's claim that field conditions caused injuries

On Sunday in Germany, both teams had key players go down with significant injuries. And both injuries could be season-ending.

After the game, Patriots defensive tackle Milton Williams blamed the injuries suffered by Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (knee) and Patriots quarterback Drake Maye (ankle) on the playing surface in Munich.

“Two top players in this league, probably got season-ending injuries playing on that [field],” Williams said, via Andrew Callahan of the Boston Herald. “It was bad. But like I said, I ain’t making no excuses. [The Lions] executed. They played on the same field we did, and they executed and made more plays than we did. Give them credit.”

On Monday, Patriots coach Mike Vrabel disagreed with Williams’s opinion.

“I don’t think Drake Maye’s injury was caused by the grass,” Vrabel told reporters, “and certainly we’ve seen other players that have celebrated or have left their feet and come down and had issues at that. So the field was the same for both teams, and that’s kind of what the field was.”

Vrabel stopped short of commenting on whether the field was an issue independent of the injuries. But he’s right; it’s the same for both teams.

Still, we know by now that every field is different. And there’s surely a difference between American fields that host up to 10 games per year every year and the international one-off surfaces.

#2728. Kyle Hamilton: Ravens are "disgracing" their "standards"

The Ravens wore all black on Monday night. It looked like they were going to a funeral.

The Ravens’ season feels over after losing their fifth in a row, following a 4-1 start. And while it’s easy to blame the free fall on the multiple injuries suffered by quarterback Lamar Jackson, new quarterback Jacoby Brissett managed to beat the Chargers with the Cardinals in Week 1. On Monday night, the Ravens were flat, lifeless, and uninspired in prime time against the previously 0-8 Chargers.

The end result was a second straight home loss in a standalone, nationally televised game.

“Right now, we are disgracing those standards, as leaders, as players and as a team,” safety Kyle Hamilton said after the game, via Jamison Hensley of ESPN. “Whatever flak we get is warranted. . . . All the talking that’s been going on is all just talk. If you don’t go out there and do what you’re supposed to do, stop the run, nobody really cares, and stuff like this just stops mattering.”

Hamilton understands that performances like this can have consequences.

“At some point, you have to just decide about your manhood and what you want out of your job,” Hamilton said. “This is our job. We can all get fired; that’s the reality of it. If we don’t do better, then we get fired.”

Hamilton has enhanced job security from his contract. Hamilton’s $17 million salary for 2027 is already fully guaranteed. And his $17 million salary for 2028 becomes fully guaranteed in early 2027.

Still, others who lack that type of financial certainty could be facing a one-way ticket out of Baltimore.

Compounding the latest loss is the fact that it came against Jim Harbaugh, the brother of longtime Ravens coach John Harbaugh and the mentor of current Ravens coach Jesse Minter. One of the explanations for the Chargers starting 0-8 was the departure of Minter, the Chargers’ defensive coordinator during Jim Harbaugh’s first two years in L.A. and his final two years at Michigan.

The embarrassing loss to the NFL’s last winless team creates an unfortunate bookend to John Harbaugh’s 18-year tenure in Baltimore. The year before he arrived, the Ravens started 4-2 before going on a losing streak that included losing to the league’s last winless team at home — the 0-13 Dolphins.

That got Brian Billick fired. And while Minter is a long way from being one and done, the Ravens need to stop the bleeding. Quickly.

The schedule isn’t favorable. They have a short-week trip to Charlotte and a visit to Houston before a Week 13 bye. The stretch run includes a home game against the Buccaneers followed by a four-game tour of the AFC North: at Steelers, Browns, at Bengals on New Year’s Eve, Steelers.

In John Harbaugh’s 18 seasons, the Ravens had fewer than eight wins only once (they were 5-11 in 2015). It’s going to be very hard to get to eight wins in 2026.

They may not get past five. Hell, they may not get to five.

Things can change quickly in the NFL. But the more the losses pile up, the harder it gets to dig out of the ever-deepening hole. For now, they’re fading into the rear-view mirror in a division with three teams that have six or more wins.

If the Ravens lose on Sunday, good luck finding the focus and the discipline during Thanksgiving week to be fully prepared for a meeting with the Texans that could be a de facto elimination game.

Even if the Ravens’ all-black uniforms was a fitting demonstration of the fact that, for 2026, they’re already dead.

#2727. Derrick Henry on 10 carries for 10 yards: I don't have an answer, just not good enough

With the Ravens starting a quarterback, Jacoby Brissett, who had been on the team for less than two weeks, they hoped to get good production from running back Derrick Henry on Monday night. That didn’t happen.

“I don’t know. I don’t have no answer. Guys who are out there are healthy, trying to do their jobs, we just ain’t good enough,” Henry said.

Henry did run for two touchdowns, but he also had five plays on which he got no gain or negative yardage. #2726. Avalanche Mailbag 2.0: Fixing the Blueline, Team Toughness & Will Avs Trade Wedgewood?

With Scott Wedgewood struggling to start the season and Ilya Nabokov already proving he's probably too good to stay in the AHL, would the Avs consider moving Wedgewood? And what sort of return could he snag, considering how great he was last year?

This is not an outlandish idea at all. Wedgewood is a pending UFA and, at some point this season, you're going to give Nabokov a look in the NHL. If he looks like a fine backup and can hold his own, and if Mackenzie Blackwood keeps playing like a solid starter, I could see Wedgewood at least being entertained as a trade option, particularly if Colorado needs additional cap space to shore up other parts of their lineup. There just needs to be a reason to move Wedgewood (like needing the cap space) because otherwise, there really isn't a point. It's better to have three capable goalies for the playoffs than just Blackwood and a rookie, even if Wedgewood has struggled.

I will say though, don't expect a big return for him if or when he gets moved. I would imagine a mid-level draft pick is the most you'd get for him if he's not involved in a multi-player trade. Goalies traditionally don't go for much and the return isn't really going to move the needle for the Avs.

Read NextAvalanche Mailbag 1.0: Road Struggles, Bednar's Approach & Can Avs Actually Get Kucherov?

Seems like everyone is set on upgrading the forward position but what do you think we need to do to fix the D core. We have been playing so poorly in front of our own net and it is killing us. Do you think this is a matter of shifting D pairs, or is it time to bring in some fresh blood?

Editor's note: This question was asked before the Makar injury and Mike Reilly waiver claim.

I think shifting the defense pairings (before the Makar injury) has helped settle things down a bit. Devon Toews' exceptional start to the season has played a big part in this. But, I'd imagine change might soon be necessary. And it's entirely contingent on how Brett Kulak and Sam Malinski look in the lead up to the trade deadline. If you determine that you need to make a move, there are a few different ways you can go.

Option 1: If you decide to load up the left side of the blueline, you can entertain moving Sam Malinski for a strong second pair LHD, which pushes Kulak to the third pair. The right side would then be Makar, Josh Manson, and Brent Burns. And your extras Noah Juulsen and perhaps Mike Reilly if sticks around all year.

Option 2: You can make a depth pickup that pushes someone, perhaps Burns, into a depth role heading into the playoffs. In this scenario, you're fine with the Kulak-Malinski pairing.

Regarding Burns, I'm still not sure exactly if they see him as a permanent piece of the lineup in the postseason (don't forget, the Ironman streak is strictly regular season). Perhaps that's something that needs to be answered in the coming months.

Elliotte Friedman said on his podcast Monday that the Avs are purposely limiting Burns' minutes and role in the early parts of the season to keep him as fresh as possible for the playoffs. Friedman then referenced the game against Montreal, when Burns played more than 19 minutes and looked good without Manson or Makar available. Obviously, with those injuries, you'll need him more. But I do wonder if Colorado's strategy is to try and flip his season to be the opposite of what it was a year ago, when he started strong and was more underwhelming as the season dragged on and into the playoffs.

Read NextAvalanche Claim Depth Defenseman From Carolina Hurricanes

Will the Avs trade for an enforcer of some kind? I know Manson and Gabe Landeskog are always willing, but the stars have been taking too many big shots the past year or two. Personally, I think there needs to be retaliation anytime any of your star guys take one, and I don't think too many guys are scared of the consequences of taking a run at Nathan MacKinnon, Makar, or Martin Necas. Example A: Leshunov the other night. What would it take to get a Olivier/Rempe/Xhekaj type in an Avs sweater?

I just don't think it's a priority to get one of those guys, especially given the build of the Avalanche roster right now. Mathieu Olivier would be a good fit, but he's not getting moved anytime soon. They love him in Columbus, as they should. But Matt Rempe and Arber Xhekaj would not be everyday players on this team. They're not fast enough nor do they bring enough (fighting aside) to the table.

I do want to push back on your notion that the Avs don't retaliate enough. When Levshunov took that run at Makar, Landeskog was going at it with him the rest of the night.

Against Detroit, we saw Nazem Kadri retaliate and go at Mo Seider, and we eventually saw Landeskog fight him to stick up for himself. As you said, Manson is never afraid to step in, and neither is Logan O'Connor. But we've even seen Martin Necas step in for Makar this season — something that wasn't a part of his game.

This team has each others' backs and I don't think they're lacking toughness, nor do I see a need for a true enforcer.

Barlow 'making progress,' Embiid out vs. Pacers, more on Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow decided to finish his post-practice workout Saturday with a windmill dunk.

While Barlow remains out with a "lower back stress reaction" listing on the Sixers' injury report, it's one sign that he's trending up in his rehab process.

"He's making progress," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He was a part of the practice today. It's kind of a game day type of practice … so it wasn't super intense, as you might expect. But he was out there doing some stuff. He's progressing. Just don't know when he'll be ready to go, but at least he's making some progress now."

Barlow was one of the Sixers' top role players last season, starting 59 games and contributing in a variety of areas. He averaged 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals in 23.8 minutes per game.

"Size, athleticism, rebounding," Dean Wade said of the fifth-year forward. "He's another guy that can switch one through five."

Whenever the 6-foot-9 Barlow is available for his season debut, the Sixers will welcome him back as another option at center.

With Ariel Hukporti's preseason Achilles tendon tear and Adem Bona's cervical spine sprain, the team is light on healthy resources at the five. And Joel Embiid (right foot plantar fascia injury management) will sit Sunday afternoon vs. the Pacers on the first leg of a back-to-back. He'll presumably return to the lineup when the Sixers visit the Nets on Monday night.

"I think (Barlow) is probably going to have to play more five than we envisioned," Nurse said, "but Jabari (Walker) is doing that as well. If the situation calls for that, I imagine that's where he'll help the most."

With the Delaware Blue Coats hosting the Maine Red Claws on Friday and Saturday, the Sixers saw an opportunity for two players outside of the regular rotation to log minutes with their G League affiliate.

Rookie guard Labaron Philon Jr. suited up Friday and third-year wing Justin Edwards played both games. Philon posted 20 points, nine rebounds and eight assists in a blowout loss. Edwards shot just 1 for 10 from the floor in the first game, but he scored 23 points on 9-for-17 shooting in Saturday's win.

Nurse has largely kept a tight rotation early in the season and the 10-3 Sixers haven't had many lopsided games with ample garbage time. As of Saturday night, the Sixers ranked 29th in the NBA in bench minutes per game.

"My first belief is it's hard to get better unless you're playing," Nurse said. "That kind of trumps everything, in my opinion. Just historically, we've had a number of guys that have gone down to those games, respected it … and it's really helped them in their careers. … It's been a minute for these guys — both of them — since they've had any real court time.

"So that fit into the schedule well. … I think it will also help them to just find some rhythm, find their legs a little bit. We're going to need everybody at some point, so keep 'em going."

VJ Edgecombe was among the crowd Friday night at Chase Fieldhouse.

"They're my brothers, man," he said. "Labaron, Justin, (Caleb Love), (Rayan Rupert). I just wanted to show love. … I didn't have (anything) to do yesterday. … We have that relationship off the court, too. I'm just being supportive and showing them that I care about them."

#2724. Lions' coach defends player celebrations after Aidan Hutchinson ACL tear

The Detroit Lions will be without their biggest star on defense for the rest of the year.

Lions head coach Dan Campbell confirmed that edge rusher Aidan Hutchinson's knee injury, which he sustained celebrating a sack on Sunday, Nov. 15, was a torn ACL. Hutchinson will miss the remainder of the season as he recovers.

"Man, what a gut punch," Campbell said on Detroit radio station 97.1 The Ticket on Tuesday, Nov. 17. "He's such a phenomenal player. This guy's an impact player, but more importantly he's an unbelievable teammate. And he's one of our captains. It just stinks, man."

The coach also said that Hutchinson's injury will not create any discussions with the Lions' players when it comes to celebrating. Campbell said he doesn't want the team to stop playing with emotion just because Hutchinson's injury came while celebrating.

"I think what happened speaks for itself," Campbell said. "We're not talking to 8-year-olds here."

He explained that he once felt as though he tore a bicep while pumping his arm in celebration, but it hasn't stopped him from showing emotion.

"This game is built off of emotion," Campbell said. "And if you have to contain yourself after a play is made – I'm not talking about in the moment, in the game, because you gotta keep your head – then you're really becoming something you're not."

"I'm not going to say, 'Nobody celebrates anymore.'"

More: Aidan Hutchinson's injury was a freak accident – and totally avoidable

Campbell confirmed that Hutchinson tore his ACL while celebrating his sack of Drake Maye. The Nov. 15 injury will knock the edge rusher out for the remainder of the season.

"I hate it, I know he hates it," Campbell said. "But I mean, the number of moves that I've seen Hutch make and the number of plyometrics, explosive movements, the spins, the one leg lateral movements, lateral balances, I'm just like, it was one of those things, man," Campbell said.

Hutchinson sustained the injury when he landed after a celebratory leap. The edge rusher's right knee buckled when his legs hit the ground again, and he fell over and grabbed at his knee.

Here's how Detroit's pass-rush attack looks now with Hutchinson now out for the season:

Hutchinson is tied for the title of Lions' most effective pass-rusher on the exterior. Wonnum, who signed a one-year deal with Detroit before the season, is tied with Hutchinson at five sacks entering Week 10.

Outside of those two, Moore is the only defensive end with more than one sack so far – he has two.

#2722. Von Miller blames himself, not the play call, for Christian McCaffrey's late TD

The Cowboys pulled within 38-32 of the 49ers with 3:42 left in the fourth quarter on Sunday. Only 19 seconds later, Christian McCaffrey was celebrating in the end zone.

Cowboys defensive coordinator Christian Parker is under fire for calling cover zero on second-and-10. He sent a six-man blitz, leaving man-to-man coverage and Von Miller matched up with McCaffrey.

McCaffrey called it “a perfect look for that play.”

The running back ran a seam route, quickly got a step (or two or three) on the 37-year-old edge rusher, caught the ball at the 40 and was off to the races. Miller reached 18.5 mph while chasing McCaffrey, but it wasn’t fast enough.

Miller, though, defended Parker and the play call on Monday.

“As a defensive coordinator in that situation, you want to be aggressive,” Miller said, via Nick Harris of the Fort Worth Star-Telegram. “We were trying to win the game. We were trying to create momentum. We were down by six points. If we could have just got off the field. . . . As a defensive coordinator, you want to be aggressive and want to win the game. That’s the type of mindset you want in these games.”

Parker is three years younger than Miller, but has Miller’s full support despite the struggles of the Cowboys defense. Dallas allowed 584 yards to the 49ers.

“Nah, that’s crazy,” Miller said of the calls for Parker’s job. “Christian is an incredible leader of men. All of these offensive and defensive coordinators are geniuses. It’s really getting your players to buy in. What happened with me yesterday is a microcosm of what has happened the whole season. Instead of me looking for a simpler way to do that job, I have to find a better way to do it. That had nothing to do with Christian Parker. I had been coached on how to play that route. I’ve had examples of that route, went over that play in practice this week. I just didn’t make that play. That had nothing to do with Christian Parker. I just didn’t execute.”

#2721. Four things to watch as Sixers look to respond against Raptors

Four things to watch as Sixers look to respond against Raptors originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers will not need to wait long for an opportunity to move past their most lopsided loss of the season.

Less than 48 hours after Boston's 123-101 win exposed several of Philadelphia's early weaknesses, the Sixers will host the Toronto Raptors on Thursday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

The Raptors are coming off a difficult meeting with the Celtics, too. Toronto led Boston by 22 points Wednesday before being outscored 61-34 over the final 29:24 of a 98-93 loss.

Philadelphia enters at 9-3 following Tuesday's defeat. Toronto is 5-6 and will complete a back-to-back and its third game in four nights.

LeBron James has been ruled out because of low-back and sciatica management. Joel Embiid is questionable with right plantar fascia injury management. Toronto will be without Kawhi Leonard, Scottie Barnes and Nate Bittle.

Here are four areas to watch as the Sixers try to respond.

Few teams have forced opponents into mistakes as frequently as Toronto.

The Raptors have generated 18.9 turnovers per game, the highest mark in the NBA. They are also averaging 10.9 steals and nearly 15 offensive rebounds.

The Sixers have countered that type of pressure well. Their 13.0 turnovers per game are tied for one of the lowest marks in the league.

Toronto's length remains disruptive even without Leonard and Barnes. The Raptors regularly crowd ball handlers, reach into passing lanes and send multiple players toward the offensive glass. Jakob Poeltl supplies size inside while RJ Barrett, Ja'Kobe Walter and Collin Murray-Boyles can create problems away from the ball.

The Raptors' pressure can punish a careless pass twice. It takes away a Sixers possession and gives Toronto an opportunity to score before the defense gets organized.

Philadelphia has largely avoided those mistakes while adjusting to a new lineup and playing at a quicker pace. Maintaining that control without James, who has handled a significant share of the playmaking, will be one of Thursday's clearest challenges.

Toronto has not built its offense around half-court shooting.

The Raptors are averaging 49.6 paint points and 16.4 fast-break points. Even while shorthanded Wednesday, they scored 18 points in transition against Boston.

That presents a direct test for a Sixers defense allowing 16.5 fast-break points per game, the fourth-highest figure in the NBA. Philadelphia has also surrendered 49.5 paint points per contest.

The first concern will be slowing the ball.

Immanuel Quickley returned Wednesday after missing two games with right hamstring soreness. He gives Toronto another guard capable of attacking early in the shot clock, while Barrett has consistently looked to get downhill with Leonard and Barnes unavailable.

Poeltl can create scoring chances without having a play called for him. He runs the floor, screens for Toronto's guards, rolls toward the rim and cleans up misses.

The Sixers have been effective in transition themselves, averaging 17.1 fast-break points. Maxey and VJ Edgecombe can change ends quickly after a stop, but Philadelphia must secure the ball first.

Thursday's game could swing on which team gets to run and which one is forced to create against a set defense.

Toronto provided an extreme example Wednesday of how quickly the three-point line can alter a game.

The Raptors entered their matchup against Boston shooting 31.8 percent from beyond the arc. They proceeded to hit 10 of their 18 attempts in the first quarter, tying a franchise record for threes in a period.

They went 6 for 25 over the final three quarters.

After Wednesday, Toronto is shooting 32.4 percent from three this season. The Sixers should not treat the Raptors like an elite perimeter team. They also cannot allow them to find early confidence through uncovered catch-and-shoot attempts.

Walter made five threes Wednesday and scored a team-high 17 points. Rookie Allen Graves added 13 points, while Quickley, Barrett and Jamison Battle are all willing shooters when the ball reaches them in rhythm.

Philadelphia's problems against Boston extended beyond the Celtics making difficult shots. The Sixers were late on several closeouts and occasionally allowed too much separation while navigating screens.

Toronto does not rely on the three as heavily as Boston. Wednesday showed it can still build a lead quickly when those shots start falling.

The Raptors expended significant energy Wednesday before traveling to Philadelphia.

They scored 38 first-quarter points, built a 22-point lead and then watched Boston erase the entire margin before the end of the third quarter. Quickley and Barrett each played more than 31 minutes. Poeltl and Walter both played at least 29.

The Sixers should have the fresher legs. Whether they take advantage could become apparent early.

Philadelphia has averaged 26.3 first-quarter points with a minus-2.2 scoring margin in opening periods. Slow starts have forced the Sixers to play from behind several times, including in their 29-point comeback against Cleveland.

An aggressive start would make Toronto respond quickly after a draining loss. Maxey and Edgecombe can apply pressure by pushing the ball after defensive rebounds instead of allowing the Raptors to set their pressure defense.

James' absence will require an adjustment. Maxey, Brown and Anfernee Simons should handle more of the ball and more of the decisions that come with initiating the offense.

If Embiid plays, the Sixers can make Toronto work through Poeltl by establishing him inside. If Embiid sits, Philadelphia's size concerns will become more pronounced with Adem Bona, Dominick Barlow and Ariel Hukporti also unavailable.

The Sixers have a rest advantage against a depleted opponent coming off an emotional loss. Their response should begin well before the fourth quarter.

#2720. Three referees will be working in other roles on Sunday, due to the ground stop

The full scope of the adjustments to the usual officiating crews isn’t officially known. It’s clear, however, that Friday’s nationwide ground stop will result in significant changes to the usual groupings of officiating crews.

On Saturday morning, the NFL announced that three non-referees will serve as referees for four Week 10 games, with one of them — Don Willard — working as the referee for the 1:00 p.m. ET game between the Dolphins and Colts before traveling to Cincinnati for Sunday night’s Steelers-Bengals game.

Via Ben Austro of FootballZebras.com, three referees will be working in other roles on Sunday.

Referee Carl Cheffers and referee Alex Kemp will work as umpires. Referee Shawn Hochuli (pictured) will work as a back judge.

The reconfiguration resulted from the NFL’s decision to assign officials to games within driving distance of their homes.

Austro also notes that the replay officials for all Sunday games will work out of NFL headquarters. They were in Detroit on Friday for an all-day, in-person training session. Instead of fanning out to the various game locations, they all went to New York.

It sets the stage for a potentially strange day, with any mistakes inevitably being blamed on the inability of the NFL to get the officials where they needed to be. If it works, however, it could prompt the NFL to reconsider the overall staffing plan, if there’s a way to do it that reduces expenses.

I think that’s a tongue-in-cheek remark. I hope it’s a tongue-in-cheek remark.

Either way, Sunday could get interesting. The players and coaches have gotten to where they’re supposed to be. The officials haven’t, and the deck has been dramatically reshuffled in order to ensure that each game will be properly staffed.

#2719. Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers

Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Kelly Oubre Jr. experience switched sides Sunday afternoon at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Back in Philadelphia with the visiting Pacers, Oubre was his usual self. There's never any risk of mistaking Oubre for somebody else.

"Love him," Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle said pregame. "Such an upbeat guy. He's such a unique person. Love the wardrobe, all the different stuff. He's a very authentically one-of-a-kind type guy."

Carlise then detailed Oubre's various basketball qualities, some of which he showed in Indiana's 131-124 loss. The 30-year-old lefty wing scored 16 points and grabbed four rebounds. His day also included a theatrical imitation of Jaylen Brown's off-arm push after the Sixers' star was whistled for an offensive foul.

Sixers fans grew familiiar with Oubre's strengths, weaknesses and eccentricities during his three seasons as a Sixer. He wound up becoming the team's third-longest tenured player behind only Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey. Oubre played 178 regular-season games and averaged 14.9 points, 5.4 rebounds, 1.6 assists and 1.3 steals.

After the Sixers replaced president of basketball operations Daryl Morey with Mike Gansey, Oubre signed a two-year contract with the Pacers in free agency.

"Mike came over from Cleveland and I heard whispers that he wanted some of his Cleveland guys," Oubre said before the game in the visitors' locker room. "And I was like, 'OK, cool. I'm a Philly guy. So I guess when he comes over, I will no longer be a Philly guy.' That was kind of the situation at hand.

"No bad blood because business is business at the end of the day. They have a great team over there, and they put together a team that they think is the best for them. I'll be all right elsewhere. I figured it out."

Oubre thought Indiana made sense as his next stop.

"It was the pace, the culture, the attitude that this team has," he said. "It's a fast-paced style of basketball. It's a bunch of guys who work collectively as a unit and kind of figure it out from there.

"I thought that would be a great opportunity for me to come and and kind of free-flow play, not have to think. I was wrong. Been thinking a lot, but I'm starting to figure it out and starting to get my rhythm here. … It's a great culture. Coach Rick Carlisle is a legend, Hall of Fame coach. And they wanted me. I love people who want me."

As Oubre settled into a new city, the Sixers had a stunning, transformational summer.

Gansey traded for Jaylen Brown, signed LeBron James, and added veteran role players like (former Cavalier) Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope. Oubre followed along.

"It was must-see TV, like (for) everybody else," he said. "As a basketball fan, it was pretty cool to see where I was kind of reinvent and get new life and new energy. So I was happy with that. Would I have loved to be a part of it? Of course.

"But at the end of the day, I'm a grown man and I've got to keep it moving and figure it out for myself. It was entertaining. … I'm kind of happy for all the people that have sacrificed over these years. … I'm trying to put a road block in that road for y'all."

Oubre joined the Sixers on a minimum-salary deal in September of 2023 after averaging 20.3 points the past season with the Hornets. Head coach Nick Nurse pitched Oubre on his potential to be a valuable, multi-dimensional player for good teams.

"I was literally about to be done with basketball," he said. "Coach Nurse called me multiple times and said, 'I want you to play for me.' And I was like, 'You know what? This guy really seems to care. He really wants me.' So thank you for just pulling me out of that pit and that hole that I was in four years ago. I got some new life in my basketball career.

"I'm always thankful for that and I love Nurse for that. Just appreciative for my time here. The battle wounds and scars that I've been able to build up, it made me a better man."

Throughout his Sixers stint, Oubre often spoke of growth, evolution, adaptation and self-discovery.

He talked again Sunday about his desire to "Be water" and how life as a Sixer changed him.

"I grew a lot, man," Oubre said. "Just the mental fortitude of being able to withstand adversity. Being able to wake up and not necessarily know which soliders are going to battle, just having to put on different hats on any given night. At first it was challenging, because I'm thinking about all the logistics and things and like, 'This is not what they asked me to do.' But at the end of the day, I just bought into being somebody that can be like water and grow with the team.

"It's been a blessing to be able to play with (VJ Edgecombe), be able to watch Tyrese (Maxey) ascend. Those are the guys where we were front line a lot. Just going through war with those guys was amazing. It just taught me mental fortitude. If I can make it through this, I can make it through anything."

Oubre felt he developed a meaningful connection with the Philadelphia fan base over his three years.

"When they talk about the City of Brotherly Love, I think they're talking about your soul, not necessarily about what you can offer them and what you do," he said. "Everybody in this city works hard. They wake up, go to work, keep the city going. And us as the prima donna basketball players, the least we can do is go hard and sacrifice for them so that they have something to cheer about when they come home from work.

"I think that was the relationship that we had. I'm forever indebted and forever grateful to the people in the city of Philadelphia."

#2718. Through 10 weeks, the AFC playoff field is basically set

The 2021 expansion of the regular season to 17 games has given the bad teams more time to separate from the good teams. Which makes it easier for the eventual playoff teams to declare themselves.

This year, the seven AFC playoff teams have essentially declared themselves after only 10 weeks of games.

The most important developments in that regard were these: (1) the Browns beating the Texans; (2) the Ravens losing to the Chargers; and (3) the Patriots losing badly to the Lions — and losing quarterback Drake Maye, possibly for the rest of the season.

Already, we know that the Bills, Jaguars, Bengals, Broncos, and Chiefs are essentially locks to win tickets to the postseason party. The Steelers, despite being outclassed by the Bengals, have enough winnable games remaining to land a wild-card berth.

The only spot up for grabs is the seven seed. And the Browns are now in the driver’s seat.

Already, four AFC teams are dead in the water: the 2-8 Jets, the bill1-8 Dolphins, the 1-8 Titans, and the 1-8 Chargers. The 3-7 Colts barely have a pulse, and it won’t last long. That’s five of 16 teams, with a fork already stuck in a spot where forks aren’t supposed to go. And the 3-6 Texans aren’t far behind.

The remaining contenders for the last spot in the playoff tree are the 6-4 Browns, the 5-5 Patriots, the 4-6 Raiders, and the 4-6 Ravens. New England could be in the process of imploding. The Raiders are 1-6 over their last seven games, and they’re now starting a rookie quarterback. The Ravens have lost five in a row.

As a result, there’s currently not much drama as to who the seven AFC playoff teams will be.

The good news is that the top of the seven-team hierarchy remains undecided — and it could stay that way for a while. The Bills, Jaguars, and Bengals stand apart from the rest. The Broncos and Chiefs remain in the mix. (The Steelers seem to be destined for another one-and-done postseason appearance, with a road game looming to Buffalo, Jacksonville, Cincinnati, or Denver/Kansas City.)

The bad news, and the inescapable reality, is that the have-nots have become crystal clear. With eight weeks of the season left.

For a league that loves parity, there isn’t much of it when it comes to the line separating the playoff teams from the wait-til-next-year franchises.
